People entrust their savings with the banks which banks handle in the form of deposits. Banks also use some of these deposits for other economic activities and therefore they give interest to the depositors.\r\n\r\nBanks may use deposits for investment in the agricultural and industrial sectors for their development or they may buy government securities or they may lend some proportion of the deposits to people who need money. When banks invest some of the deposits, they earn profit and when they lend this money, they earn by charging interest.\r\n\r\nThe lending rate of interest is higher than the rate of interest which banks give on deposits and the difference between the two is the profit earning. Hence, banks are called commercial institutions.\r\n\r\nIn other words, banks undertake activity of mobilizing peoples' money in order to make profit and therefore they are commercial institutions.\r\n\r\n# Functions of Commercial Banks\r\n\r\nCommercial banks perform several functions which are as under:\r\n\r\n## Primary Functions\r\n\r\n(A) Primary Functions:\r\n\r\n### Accepting Deposits\r\n\r\n1.  Accepting Deposits: A bank accepts money from the people (savers) in the form of deposits. It gives safety to the deposits of its customers by acting as a custodian of funds. In a way people lend their savings to banks for which banks give them interest.\r\n\r\nDeposits are mainly of three types:\r\n\r\n#### Current Account Deposits\r\n\r\n(1) Current Account Deposits: A current account is opened with a bank in the name of a business, firm or an individual. The deposits in this account are more liquid than deposits in any other account. Withdrawal can be made from this account any number of times during a day. The customers of this account are given a cheque book but do not get interest on current account deposits, on the contrary certain times banks may impose some kind of service charges on this account. A customer may even obtain overdraft facility for business purpose on this account as per rules of the bank.\r\n\r\n#### Savings Account Deposits\r\n\r\n(2) Savings Account Deposits: People usually deposit savings for a short period in this account for which banks give them interest. Upon requirement, they can withdraw with a cheque or withdrawal slip. In present times they can also withdraw money from this account by using debit card or credit card.\r\n\r\n##### Facility of Recurring Account (Recurring Deposit)\r\n\r\nFacility of Recurring Account (Recurring Deposit): These deposits are also a type of savings deposits. People who do not wish to save or cannot save bigger sums of money, save and deposit small amounts each month for some period of time in this account. Thus, the savings gradually increase and interest is paid on the accumulated money. Thus, these deposits are called recurring deposits. (By rules of some banks, if a person skips to deposit some month's money in this account, she\/he may have to pay a penalty along with some interest loss.)\r\n\r\n#### Fixed\/Long Term Deposits\r\n\r\n*   Fixed\/Long Term Deposits: These deposits are for a fixed period of time and usually people who want to deposit money for a long period of time choose such deposits. Banks pay highest rate of interest on such deposits among all types of deposits. Customers get overdraft facilities on such deposits.\r\n\r\n### Providing Credit Facilities\r\n\r\n(2) Providing Credit Facilities: When individuals or businesses require money for personal or investment purposes, they borrow from banks and banks provide credit by charging a rate of interest. In terms of time period, borrowing\/credit can be for short term, medium term or long term. (These time periods are not strictly fixed and may vary for different activities. However, in a theoretical sense, short term is a period up to 1 year; medium term is a period between 1 and 5 years and long term is a period between 5 and 15 years.)\r\n\r\nIn the context of purpose, credit can be for personal reasons, for agricultural activity or for business activity.\r\n\r\nThe rates of interest charged vary with purpose.\r\n\r\n### Payment and Withdrawal Facilities\r\n\r\n(3) Payment and Withdrawal Facilities: A bank provides easy payment and withdrawal facility to its customers in the form of cheques, withdrawal slips and drafts, pay order, ATM facilities, (Automatic Teller Machines), credit and debit cards, internet banking, etc.\r\n\r\n### Credit Creation\r\n\r\n(4) Credit Creation: Banks undertake the activity of credit creation to ensure that the supply of money in the economy fulfils the demand for money. Banks create money from the existing stock of money (deposits); thereby add to the supply of money in the economy. When credit creation by banks increases, the supply of money increases and when credit creation decreases, the supply of money reduces.\r\n\r\nWhen a bank lends money from primary deposits, the borrower has to open an account with the same bank or another branch in order to deposit the cheque of the loan (borrowed sum of money). When the cheque is credited in the new account, it becomes a derived deposit for the bank. From this derived deposit, the bank can create still another loan account for a third person. The third person's loan becomes one more deposit for the bank. Thus, a bank can create several deposits from a primary deposit.\r\n\r\nBesides, when the borrower withdraws the loan amount from her\/his account and spends it, there is greater circulation\/supply of money in the economy.\r\n\r\nNo bank can lend the full amount of a deposit. From every deposit, the bank has to keep aside some proportion of money in the form of cash reserves which is known as cash reserve ratio. Thus they can lend the remaining money only.\r\n\r\nIf a bank has a primary deposit of $\u20b9 1,000$ and the cash reserve ratio determined by rule is $20 \\%$ then the bank can create credit up to $\u20b9 1,000 \\times \\frac{1}{20 \\%}$. In other words, primary deposit of $\u20b9 1,000$ can create a money supply of $\u20b9 5,000$ and it can be said that new credit of $\u20b9 4,000$ is created. This can be understood with an example,\r\n\r\nTable 4.1\r\n\r\n| Bank   (1) | Net Deposits  (in \u20b9)  (2) | Required Reserves  (in \u20b9) Reserve ratio 20 %  (3) | New Loans  (in \u20b9)  (4) |\r\n| :------: | :-----------------: | :---------------------------------: | :----------------: |\r\n|    A     |   **1,000**   |                200                |        800         |\r\n|    B     |        800        |                160                |        640         |\r\n|    C     |        640        |                128                |        512         |\r\n|  $\\cdot$  |       $\\cdot$       |               $\\cdot$               |      $\\cdot$      |\r\n|  $\\cdot$  |       $\\cdot$       |               $\\cdot$               |      $\\cdot$      |\r\n|  $\\cdot$  |       $\\cdot$       |               $\\cdot$               |      $\\cdot$      |\r\n|  Total   |        5,000        |              **1,000**              |       4,000        |\r\n\r\nThis, credit creation $=$ Primary deposit $\\times \\frac{1}{\\text { cash reserve ratio }}$\r\n\r\nWhen customers withdraw more money than the deposited amount from their current account it is called over draft facility and this also leads to credit creation.\r\n\r\n### Inter-Banking Transactions\r\n\r\n(5) Inter-Banking Transactions: Banks provide short term and long term credit to one another from time to time. Short term credit is provided by one bank to another through the central bank and is called 'call money'. The interest rate on call money is called the 'call money rate'.\r\n\r\n## Secondary\/Other Functions of Commercial Banks\r\n\r\n(B) Secondary\/Other Functions of Commercial Banks:\r\n\r\nBanks also perform certain other functions as,\r\n\r\n### Agency and Utility Services\r\n\r\n(1) Agency and Utility Services: A bank provides various utility and agent banking facilities to its customers. For example, a bank can act as a mediating agent by providing a letter of credit to the exporter on behalf of the importer. Banks also provide underwriting services to various businesses.\r\n\r\nBanks also provide facilities to its customers to pay tax challans. Banks also provide safe deposit vaults on rent to its customers, wherein they can safely store their precious jewellery, documents etc.\r\n\r\nBanks provide micro finance facilities for very small businesses which are important for development of communities. When some payments are to be made with full proof security, banks provide service to the payer by issuing draft or pay order.\r\n\r\n### Provide various Facilities with Changing Times\r\n\r\n(2) Provide various Facilities with Changing Times: Banking is an evolutionary concept. There is continuous expansion and diversification of the functions, activities and services of the banks. In present times, electronic transfer of money from the account of one customer to that of any other customer of any bank (without using cash or cheque) is done through facilities like NEFT (National Electronic Fund Transfer) and RTGS (Real Time Gross Settlement). Both these are possible because of CORE banking. (Centralized Online Real Time Exchange)\r\n\r\nBesides, customers can get various details of their account as well as make payments, buy goods, book tickets etc. on their computer or mobile phone through internet banking and mobile banking app. Banks also provide safe deposit vaults and facilities of DEMAT account. (Dematerialized account). This is an account where shares, debentures, bonds etc. can be held in electronic form which is easier than holding them in physical form.\r\n\n\nAdditional Instructions:\n","timestamp":"2025-07-20 13:34:17"},{"sender":"user","message":"","timestamp":"2025-07-20 13:34:46"},{"sender":"ai","message":"# Definition of Commercial Bank\n\nAccording to the **Banking Company Act, 1949**, a **commercial bank** is defined as an institution that \"**transacts the business of banking, that is, accepting deposits from the people for the purpose of lending or investment; repayable on demand or otherwise and withdrawable by cheque, draft, pay-order or otherwise.**\"\n\nA commercial bank operates as a **business unit** whose primary objective is to provide various **banking services** while earning **profit**. People entrust their **savings** with banks, which are managed as **deposits**. Banks provide **interest** to depositors for these funds.\n\nBanks strategically utilize these **deposits** for various **economic activities**. They may **invest** in critical sectors like agriculture and industry for development, purchase **government securities**, or **lend** a portion of these funds to individuals or businesses in need. Banks generate **profit** through their investment activities and by charging a higher **rate of interest** on loans than the interest they pay on deposits. This difference between the **lending rate** and the **deposit rate** constitutes their profit, affirming their role as **commercial institutions**. In essence, banks undertake the crucial activity of **mobilizing people's money** to generate profit.\n\n**MEMORY TIP:** Think of \"D.I.P.\" - **D**eposits are accepted, **I**nterest is paid and charged, resulting in **P**rofit.\n\n---\n\n# Functions of Commercial Banks\n\nCommercial banks perform a multitude of functions, broadly categorized into primary and secondary functions.\n\n## Primary Functions\n\n### Accepting Deposits\n\nBanks serve as a crucial facility for **accepting money** from **savers** in the form of **deposits**. They act as a **custodian of funds**, ensuring the safety and security of their customers' money. In return for lending their savings to the bank, customers receive **interest**.\n\nDeposits are broadly classified into three main types:\n\n#### Current Account Deposits\n\nA **current account** is typically opened by businesses, firms, or individuals who require frequent transactions. Deposits in this account are highly **liquid**, allowing for **unlimited withdrawals** any number of times during a single day. Customers are provided with a **cheque book**. Notably, banks **do not pay interest** on current account deposits; instead, they may levy **service charges**. A significant feature for businesses is the **overdraft facility**, which allows customers to withdraw more money than available in their account, up to a pre-approved limit, for business purposes.\n\n**MEMORY TIP:** \"CU LION\" - **Cu**rrent, **L**iquid, **I**nterest (no), **O**verdraft, **N**o limit on withdrawals.\n\n#### Savings Account Deposits\n\nIndividuals commonly use **savings accounts** to deposit funds for **short periods**. Banks provide **interest** on these deposits. Funds can be withdrawn using a **cheque**, a **withdrawal slip**, or, in modern banking, through **debit cards** or **credit cards**.\n\n**MEMORY TIP:** \"SAVE INTEREST\" - Deposit **save**d money for a short term to earn **interest**.\n\n##### Facility of Recurring Account (Recurring Deposit)\n\n**Recurring deposits** are a specialized type of savings account designed for individuals who prefer or need to save smaller, regular amounts. Customers deposit a fixed **small amount each month** for a predetermined period. The savings gradually accumulate, and **interest** is paid on the total accumulated money. Some banks may impose a **penalty** and reduce interest if monthly deposits are skipped.\n\n**MEMORY TIP:** Think of \"SIP\" - **S**ystematic **I**nvestment **P**lan, where small, regular deposits lead to accumulated savings.\n\n#### Fixed\/Long Term Deposits\n\n**Fixed deposits** (also known as **Long Term Deposits**) are chosen by individuals who wish to deposit money for a **fixed, longer period**. Among all deposit types, banks offer the **highest rate of interest** on these deposits due to their longer tenure. Customers can also avail **overdraft facilities** against their fixed deposits.\n\n**MEMORY TIP:** \"FIX High ROI\" - **Fixed** for a long term yields **High R**ate **O**f **I**nterest.\n\n### Providing Credit Facilities\n\nBanks play a crucial role in providing **credit facilities** (loans) to individuals and businesses for personal or investment needs. They earn income by **charging a rate of interest** on these loans. Credit can be categorized by its **time period**:\n*   **Short term:** Up to 1 year.\n*   **Medium term:** Between 1 and 5 years.\n*   **Long term:** Between 5 and 15 years.\nThe **rates of interest charged** vary depending on the purpose of the loan, whether it is for personal reasons, agricultural activities, or business ventures.\n\n**MEMORY TIP:** \"C.I.L.T.\" - **C**redit, **I**nterest, **L**ending, **T**erm (short, medium, long).\n\n### Payment and Withdrawal Facilities\n\nBanks offer a wide array of convenient **payment and withdrawal facilities** to their customers. These include traditional methods like **cheques**, **withdrawal slips**, **drafts**, and **pay orders**, as well as modern electronic options such as **ATM facilities** (Automated Teller Machines), **credit and debit cards**, and **internet banking**. These facilities ensure ease and accessibility of funds.\n\n**MEMORY TIP:** \"P.A.Y. F.A.S.T.\" - **P**ayment **A**nd **Y**ield (withdrawal) **F**acilities **A**re **S**imple and **T**echnological.\n\n### Credit Creation\n\nOne of the most significant functions of commercial banks is **credit creation**. This activity ensures that the **supply of money** in the economy is adequate to meet its demand. Banks **create money** by leveraging existing deposits, thereby adding to the overall money supply. An increase in credit creation leads to an increased money supply, and vice-versa.\n\nThe process of credit creation works as follows:\n1.  When a bank lends money from a **primary deposit**, the borrower often deposits the loan amount (received as a cheque) into an account, either with the same bank or another branch.\n2.  This deposited loan amount becomes a **derived deposit** for the bank.\n3.  From this derived deposit, the bank can create yet another loan for a third party, which again becomes a deposit for the bank.\n4.  This sequential process allows a bank to create multiple deposits from a single primary deposit.\nFurthermore, when borrowers withdraw and spend the loan amount, it increases the circulation and supply of money in the economy.\n\nIt is crucial to understand that no bank can lend the full amount of a deposit. A certain proportion of every deposit must be kept aside as **cash reserves**, known as the **cash reserve ratio (CRR)**, as mandated by the central bank. Banks can only lend the remaining amount.\n\n**Example Calculation of Credit Creation:**\nIf a bank receives a **primary deposit** of $\u20b9 1,000$ and the **cash reserve ratio** (CRR) is $20 \\%$, the potential credit creation can be calculated as:\n\n`Credit creation = Primary deposit $\\times \\frac{1}{\\text{cash reserve ratio}}$`\n\n`Credit creation = $\u20b9 1,000 \\times \\frac{1}{20\\%}$ = $\u20b9 1,000 \\times \\frac{1}{0.20}$ = $\u20b9 5,000$`\n\nThis implies that a primary deposit of $\u20b9 1,000$ can generate a total money supply of $\u20b9 5,000$. The **new credit created** is $\u20b9 4,000$ ($\u20b9 5,000 - \u20b9 1,000$).\n\nThe process can be further illustrated with the following table:\n\n| Bank | Net Deposits (in \u20b9) | Required Reserves (in \u20b9) Reserve ratio 20 % | New Loans (in \u20b9) |\n| :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |\n| A | **1,000** | 200 | 800 |\n| B | 800 | 160 | 640 |\n| C | 640 | 128 | 512 |\n| $\\cdot$ | $\\cdot$ | $\\cdot$ | $\\cdot$ |\n| $\\cdot$ | $\\cdot$ | $\\cdot$ | $\\cdot$ |\n| $\\cdot$ | $\\cdot$ | $\\cdot$ | $\\cdot$ |\n| Total | 5,000 | **1,000** | 4,000 |\n\nAdditionally, when customers utilize the **overdraft facility** (withdrawing more money than their deposited amount from their current account), this also contributes to **credit creation**.\n\n**MEMORY TIP:** \"C.R.R. D.M.S.\" - **C**redit **R**eation **R**equires considering the **D**erived **M**oney **S**upply and **CRR**.\n\n### Inter-Banking Transactions\n\nCommercial banks also engage in **inter-banking transactions**, providing **short-term and long-term credit** to one another as needed. Short-term credit exchanged between banks, facilitated through the central bank, is specifically termed **'call money'**. The interest rate charged on such short-term inter-bank loans is known as the **'call money rate'**.\n\n**MEMORY TIP:** \"B2B Call\" - **B**ank **to** **B**ank lending, often for short periods and called \"**Call** money\".\n\n---\n\n## Secondary\/Other Functions of Commercial Banks\n\nIn addition to their primary roles, commercial banks perform several other vital functions:\n\n### Agency and Utility Services\n\nBanks offer a diverse range of **agency and utility services** to their customers. These include:\n*   Acting as a **mediating agent**, for example, by providing a **letter of credit** to an exporter on behalf of an importer, facilitating international trade.\n*   Providing **underwriting services** to various businesses, helping them raise capital by guaranteeing the sale of securities.\n*   Facilitating the payment of **tax challans** for customers.\n*   Renting out **safe deposit vaults** where customers can securely store valuable items like jewellery and important documents.\n*   Offering **micro finance facilities** to very small businesses, contributing to community development and financial inclusion.\n*   Issuing **drafts** or **pay orders** to customers to ensure secure and proof-backed payments.\n\n**MEMORY TIP:** \"L.U.S.H. M.D.\" - **L**etter of credit, **U**nderwriting, **S**afe deposit vaults, **H**elp with tax challans, **M**icro-finance, **D**raft\/Pay order.\n\n### Provide various Facilities with Changing Times\n\nBanking is an **evolutionary concept**, continuously undergoing expansion and diversification of its functions, activities, and services. In contemporary times, banks have embraced technological advancements to offer new facilities:\n*   **Electronic transfer of money** without the need for cash or cheques is facilitated through systems like **NEFT** (**National Electronic Fund Transfer**) and **RTGS** (**Real Time Gross Settlement**). Both these systems are powered by **CORE banking** (**Centralized Online Real Time Exchange**), which allows real-time processing of transactions across branches.\n*   Customers can access account details, make payments, purchase goods, and book tickets using **internet banking** and **mobile banking apps** on their computers or smartphones.\n*   Banks also provide **DEMAT accounts** (**Dematerialized accounts**), which enable the holding of shares, debentures, bonds, and other securities in electronic form, offering greater convenience and security than physical certificates.\n\n**MEMORY TIP:** \"E.N.E.R.G.Y. People entrust their savings with the banks which banks handle in the form of deposits. Banks also use some of these deposits for other economic activities and therefore they give interest to the depositors.\r\n\r\nBanks may use deposits for investment in the agricultural and industrial sectors for their development or they may buy government securities or they may lend some proportion of the deposits to people who need money. When banks invest some of the deposits, they earn profit and when they lend this money, they earn by charging interest.\r\n\r\nThe lending rate of interest is higher than the rate of interest which banks give on deposits and the difference between the two is the profit earning. Hence, banks are called commercial institutions.\r\n\r\nIn other words, banks undertake activity of mobilizing peoples' money in order to make profit and therefore they are commercial institutions.\r\n\r\n# Functions of Commercial Banks\r\n\r\nCommercial banks perform several functions which are as under:\r\n\r\n## Primary Functions\r\n\r\n(A) Primary Functions:\r\n\r\n### Accepting Deposits\r\n\r\n1.  Accepting Deposits: A bank accepts money from the people (savers) in the form of deposits. It gives safety to the deposits of its customers by acting as a custodian of funds. In a way people lend their savings to banks for which banks give them interest.\r\n\r\nDeposits are mainly of three types:\r\n\r\n#### Current Account Deposits\r\n\r\n(1) Current Account Deposits: A current account is opened with a bank in the name of a business, firm or an individual. The deposits in this account are more liquid than deposits in any other account. Withdrawal can be made from this account any number of times during a day. The customers of this account are given a cheque book but do not get interest on current account deposits, on the contrary certain times banks may impose some kind of service charges on this account. A customer may even obtain overdraft facility for business purpose on this account as per rules of the bank.\r\n\r\n#### Savings Account Deposits\r\n\r\n(2) Savings Account Deposits: People usually deposit savings for a short period in this account for which banks give them interest. Upon requirement, they can withdraw with a cheque or withdrawal slip. In present times they can also withdraw money from this account by using debit card or credit card.\r\n\r\n##### Facility of Recurring Account (Recurring Deposit)\r\n\r\nFacility of Recurring Account (Recurring Deposit): These deposits are also a type of savings deposits. People who do not wish to save or cannot save bigger sums of money, save and deposit small amounts each month for some period of time in this account. Thus, the savings gradually increase and interest is paid on the accumulated money. Thus, these deposits are called recurring deposits. (By rules of some banks, if a person skips to deposit some month's money in this account, she\/he may have to pay a penalty along with some interest loss.)\r\n\r\n#### Fixed\/Long Term Deposits\r\n\r\n*   Fixed\/Long Term Deposits: These deposits are for a fixed period of time and usually people who want to deposit money for a long period of time choose such deposits. Banks pay highest rate of interest on such deposits among all types of deposits. Customers get overdraft facilities on such deposits.\r\n\r\n### Providing Credit Facilities\r\n\r\n(2) Providing Credit Facilities: When individuals or businesses require money for personal or investment purposes, they borrow from banks and banks provide credit by charging a rate of interest. In terms of time period, borrowing\/credit can be for short term, medium term or long term. (These time periods are not strictly fixed and may vary for different activities. However, in a theoretical sense, short term is a period up to 1 year; medium term is a period between 1 and 5 years and long term is a period between 5 and 15 years.)\r\n\r\nIn the context of purpose, credit can be for personal reasons, for agricultural activity or for business activity.\r\n\r\nThe rates of interest charged vary with purpose.\r\n\r\n### Payment and Withdrawal Facilities\r\n\r\n(3) Payment and Withdrawal Facilities: A bank provides easy payment and withdrawal facility to its customers in the form of cheques, withdrawal slips and drafts, pay order, ATM facilities, (Automatic Teller Machines), credit and debit cards, internet banking, etc.\r\n\r\n### Credit Creation\r\n\r\n(4) Credit Creation: Banks undertake the activity of credit creation to ensure that the supply of money in the economy fulfils the demand for money. Banks create money from the existing stock of money (deposits); thereby add to the supply of money in the economy. When credit creation by banks increases, the supply of money increases and when credit creation decreases, the supply of money reduces.\r\n\r\nWhen a bank lends money from primary deposits, the borrower has to open an account with the same bank or another branch in order to deposit the cheque of the loan (borrowed sum of money). When the cheque is credited in the new account, it becomes a derived deposit for the bank. From this derived deposit, the bank can create still another loan account for a third person. The third person's loan becomes one more deposit for the bank. Thus, a bank can create several deposits from a primary deposit.\r\n\r\nBesides, when the borrower withdraws the loan amount from her\/his account and spends it, there is greater circulation\/supply of money in the economy.\r\n\r\nNo bank can lend the full amount of a deposit. From every deposit, the bank has to keep aside some proportion of money in the form of cash reserves which is known as cash reserve ratio. Thus they can lend the remaining money only.\r\n\r\nIf a bank has a primary deposit of $\u20b9 1,000$ and the cash reserve ratio determined by rule is $20 \\%$ then the bank can create credit up to $\u20b9 1,000 \\times \\frac{1}{20 \\%}$. In other words, primary deposit of $\u20b9 1,000$ can create a money supply of $\u20b9 5,000$ and it can be said that new credit of $\u20b9 4,000$ is created. This can be understood with an example,\r\n\r\nTable 4.1\r\n\r\n| Bank   (1) | Net Deposits  (in \u20b9)  (2) | Required Reserves  (in \u20b9) Reserve ratio 20 %  (3) | New Loans  (in \u20b9)  (4) |\r\n| :------: | :-----------------: | :---------------------------------: | :----------------: |\r\n|    A     |   **1,000**   |                200                |        800         |\r\n|    B     |        800        |                160                |        640         |\r\n|    C     |        640        |                128                |        512         |\r\n|  $\\cdot$  |       $\\cdot$       |               $\\cdot$               |      $\\cdot$      |\r\n|  $\\cdot$  |       $\\cdot$       |               $\\cdot$               |      $\\cdot$      |\r\n|  $\\cdot$  |       $\\cdot$       |               $\\cdot$               |      $\\cdot$      |\r\n|  Total   |        5,000        |              **1,000**              |       4,000        |\r\n\r\nThis, credit creation $=$ Primary deposit $\\times \\frac{1}{\\text { cash reserve ratio }}$\r\n\r\nWhen customers withdraw more money than the deposited amount from their current account it is called over draft facility and this also leads to credit creation.\r\n\r\n### Inter-Banking Transactions\r\n\r\n(5) Inter-Banking Transactions: Banks provide short term and long term credit to one another from time to time. Short term credit is provided by one bank to another through the central bank and is called 'call money'. The interest rate on call money is called the 'call money rate'.\r\n\r\n## Secondary\/Other Functions of Commercial Banks\r\n\r\n(B) Secondary\/Other Functions of Commercial Banks:\r\n\r\nBanks also perform certain other functions as,\r\n\r\n### Agency and Utility Services\r\n\r\n(1) Agency and Utility Services: A bank provides various utility and agent banking facilities to its customers. For example, a bank can act as a mediating agent by providing a letter of credit to the exporter on behalf of the importer. Banks also provide underwriting services to various businesses.\r\n\r\nBanks also provide facilities to its customers to pay tax challans. Banks also provide safe deposit vaults on rent to its customers, wherein they can safely store their precious jewellery, documents etc.\r\n\r\nBanks provide micro finance facilities for very small businesses which are important for development of communities. When some payments are to be made with full proof security, banks provide service to the payer by issuing draft or pay order.\r\n\r\n### Provide various Facilities with Changing Times\r\n\r\n(2) Provide various Facilities with Changing Times: Banking is an evolutionary concept. There is continuous expansion and diversification of the functions, activities and services of the banks. In present times, electronic transfer of money from the account of one customer to that of any other customer of any bank (without using cash or cheque) is done through facilities like NEFT (National Electronic Fund Transfer) and RTGS (Real Time Gross Settlement). Both these are possible because of CORE banking. (Centralized Online Real Time Exchange)\r\n\r\nBesides, customers can get various details of their account as well as make payments, buy goods, book tickets etc. on their computer or mobile phone through internet banking and mobile banking app. Banks also provide safe deposit vaults and facilities of DEMAT account. (Dematerialized account). This is an account where shares, debentures, bonds etc. can be held in electronic form which is easier than holding them in physical form.\r\n\n\nAdditional Instructions:\n","timestamp":"2025-07-21 17:32:33"},{"sender":"user","message":"","timestamp":"2025-07-21 17:33:04"},{"sender":"ai","message":"# Definition of Commercial Bank\n\nAccording to the **Banking Company Act, 1949**, a **Commercial Bank** is defined as \"one which transacts the business of banking, that is, **accepting deposits** from the people for the purpose of **lending** or **investment**; repayable on demand or otherwise and withdrawable by cheque, draft, pay-order or otherwise.\"\n\nA commercial bank operates as a **business unit** whose primary objective is to provide **banking services** for **profit**. People entrust their **savings** with banks, which are managed as **deposits**. Banks provide **interest** to depositors for the use of their funds.\n\nBanks strategically utilize these deposits for various **economic activities**. They may **invest** in critical sectors like **agriculture** and **industry** to foster development, purchase **government securities**, or **lend** money to individuals and businesses in need. The bank generates **profit** from these investments and by charging **interest** on loans. Crucially, the **lending rate of interest** is consistently higher than the **rate of interest** offered on deposits. The difference between these two rates constitutes the bank's **profit**. This profit-oriented approach is why banks are referred to as **commercial institutions**.\n\nIn essence, commercial banks serve as **commercial institutions** that undertake the vital activity of **mobilizing people's money** with the ultimate goal of generating **profit**.\n\n---\n\n## Functions of Commercial Banks\n\nCommercial banks perform a multitude of functions, categorized primarily into **Primary Functions** and **Secondary\/Other Functions**.\n\n### Primary Functions\n\nPrimary functions are the core activities essential to a commercial bank's operation.\n\n#### Accepting Deposits\n\nBanks accept money from individuals and entities (referred to as **savers**) in the form of **deposits**. By doing so, they provide a crucial service: acting as a **custodian of funds**, ensuring the safety and security of their customers' money. In return for holding these savings, banks pay **interest** to the depositors.\n\nDeposits are broadly classified into three main types:\n\n##### Current Account Deposits\n\n*   **Definition:** A **current account** is typically opened by businesses, firms, or individuals who require frequent transactions.\n*   **Liquidity:** These deposits are the **most liquid** compared to other account types, allowing for unlimited withdrawals within a single day.\n*   **Facilities:** Customers receive a **cheque book** for transactions.\n*   **Interest & Charges:** Unlike other accounts, **no interest** is paid on current account deposits. In fact, banks may levy **service charges**.\n*   **Overdraft Facility:** For business purposes, customers can often avail an **overdraft facility** as per bank rules, allowing them to withdraw more money than available in their account.\n\n**MEMORY TIP:** Think of **C**urrent **A**ccount **D**eposits as being for **C**ompanies, offering **A**ll-day liquidity, but with **D**rawbacks like no interest and potential charges.\n\n##### Savings Account Deposits\n\n*   **Definition:** People primarily use **savings accounts** to deposit their savings for relatively **short periods**.\n*   **Interest:** Banks provide **interest** on the money held in these accounts.\n*   **Withdrawal Methods:** Funds can be withdrawn using a **cheque**, a **withdrawal slip**, or increasingly, through **debit cards** or **credit cards** (for cash advances or purchases against available balance).\n\n**MEMORY TIP:** **S**avings **A**ccounts are for **S**mall-scale, **S**hort-term savings, earning **S**ome interest, and accessible via **S**lips or **S**mart cards.\n\n###### Facility of Recurring Account (Recurring Deposit)\n\n*   **Definition:** These are a specialized type of **savings deposit** designed for individuals who prefer or are only able to save **small amounts** regularly.\n*   **Mechanism:** Customers deposit a fixed small sum each month for a predetermined period.\n*   **Growth & Interest:** Over time, these small, consistent deposits gradually accumulate, and **interest** is paid on the total accumulated money.\n*   **Penalty Clause:** Some banks may impose a **penalty** along with a loss of interest if a monthly deposit is skipped.\n\n**MEMORY TIP:** **R**ecurring **D**eposits are like **R**egular, **R**epeatable savings payments that **R**ecently grow and earn **R**ewarding interest.\n\n##### Fixed\/Long Term Deposits\n\n*   **Definition:** These deposits are made for a **fixed period of time**, typically chosen by individuals or entities looking to deposit money for an **extended duration**.\n*   **Interest Rate:** Banks offer the **highest rate of interest** on **fixed deposits** compared to all other types of deposits.\n*   **Overdraft Facility:** Customers can also avail **overdraft facilities** against their fixed deposits.\n\n**MEMORY TIP:** **F**ixed **D**eposits are for **F**irm, **F**uture financial goals, offering the **F**inest interest rates.\n\n---\n\n#### Providing Credit Facilities\n\nCommercial banks play a vital role in the economy by **providing credit facilities** (loans) to individuals and businesses. When individuals or businesses need money for personal use or investment purposes, they **borrow** from banks, and in return, banks charge a **rate of interest**.\n\n*   **Time Period Classification:**\n    *   **Short term:** Loans for a period of up to 1 year.\n    *   **Medium term:** Loans for a period between 1 and 5 years.\n    *   **Long term:** Loans for a period between 5 and 15 years.\n    (Note: These time periods are theoretical and can vary based on specific activities and bank policies.)\n*   **Purpose Classification:** Credit can be extended for various purposes, including:\n    *   **Personal reasons**\n    *   **Agricultural activity**\n    *   **Business activity**\n*   **Interest Rate Variation:** The **rates of interest charged** on loans often vary depending on the purpose for which the credit is taken.\n\n**MEMORY TIP:** **C**redit **F**acilities are for **C**apital needs, available for **C**onsumers and **C**ompanies, with **C**hanging interest based on duration and purpose.\n\n---\n\n#### Payment and Withdrawal Facilities\n\nBanks offer a wide array of convenient **payment and withdrawal facilities** to their customers, ensuring easy access to funds and smooth transactions.\n\n*   **Traditional Methods:**\n    *   **Cheques**\n    *   **Withdrawal slips**\n    *   **Drafts**\n    *   **Pay orders**\n*   **Modern Electronic Methods:**\n    *   **ATM facilities** (Automatic Teller Machines)\n    *   **Credit and Debit cards**\n    *   **Internet banking**\n    *   **Mobile banking**\n\n**MEMORY TIP:** **P**ayment & **W**ithdrawal facilities enable **P**eople to **W**ithdraw easily using **P**aper (cheques) or **W**ireless (cards, online) methods.\n\n---\n\n#### Credit Creation\n\n**Credit creation** is a unique and fundamental function of commercial banks, where they expand the **money supply** in the economy beyond the initial deposits. Banks achieve this by creating new money from the existing stock of money (deposits).\n\n*   **Mechanism of Credit Creation:**\n    1.  When a bank grants a loan from a **primary deposit**, the borrower typically deposits the loan amount (received as a cheque) into an account, either with the same bank or another branch.\n    2.  This new deposit becomes a **derived deposit** for the bank.\n    3.  From this **derived deposit**, the bank can then issue another loan, which in turn becomes another derived deposit for the banking system.\n    4.  This process allows a bank to create multiple derived deposits from a single **primary deposit**, significantly multiplying the initial money.\n*   **Impact on Money Supply:** When bank **credit creation increases**, the **supply of money** in the economy expands. Conversely, a decrease in credit creation leads to a reduction in the money supply.\n*   **Circulation of Money:** When a borrower spends the loan amount, it leads to greater circulation and supply of money in the economy.\n*   **Cash Reserve Ratio (CRR):** Banks are legally required to hold a certain proportion of every deposit as **cash reserves**. This is known as the **Cash Reserve Ratio (CRR)**. Banks can only lend out the remaining money after setting aside this reserve.\n\n*   **Formula for Credit Creation:**\n    Credit creation = Primary deposit $\\times \\frac{1}{\\text{cash reserve ratio}}$\n\n*   **Example:**\n    If a bank receives a **primary deposit of $\u20b9 1,000$** and the **Cash Reserve Ratio (CRR) is $20 \\%$**, the bank can potentially create credit up to:\n    $\u20b9 1,000 \\times \\frac{1}{20\\%} = \u20b9 1,000 \\times \\frac{1}{0.20} = \u20b9 5,000$\n    This means the initial $\u20b9 1,000$ deposit can lead to a total money supply of $\u20b9 5,000$, implying a **new credit creation of $\u20b9 4,000$**.\n\nHere's how the process unfolds in a simplified banking system:\n\n| Bank (1) | Net Deposits (in \u20b9) (2) | Required Reserves (in \u20b9) Reserve ratio 20 % (3) | New Loans (in \u20b9) (4) |\n| :-------: | :-----------------: | :---------------------------------: | :----------------: |\n| A | **1,000** | 200 | 800 |\n| B | 800 | 160 | 640 |\n| C | 640 | 128 | 512 |\n| $\\cdot$ | $\\cdot$ | $\\cdot$ | $\\cdot$ |\n| $\\cdot$ | $\\cdot$ | $\\cdot$ | $\\cdot$ |\n| $\\cdot$ | $\\cdot$ | $\\cdot$ | $\\cdot$ |\n| Total | 5,000 | **1,000** | 4,000 |\n\n*   **Overdraft Facility and Credit Creation:** The **overdraft facility**, where customers withdraw more than their current account balance, also contributes to credit creation by expanding the money supply.\n\n**MEMORY TIP:** **C**redit **C**reation is the **C**ore way banks **C**irculate money by **C**reating **C**onsecutive loans from initial deposits, limited by **C**RR.\n\n---\n\n#### Inter-Banking Transactions\n\nCommercial banks frequently engage in **inter-banking transactions**, providing **short-term** and **long-term credit** to one another as needed.\n\n*   **Call Money:** Short-term credit between banks is often facilitated through the **Central Bank** and is specifically known as **'call money'**.\n*   **Call Money Rate:** The **interest rate** charged on these call money loans is referred to as the **'call money rate'**.\n\n**MEMORY TIP:** **I**nter-**B**anking **T**ransactions are simply banks **I**nteracting to **B**orrow and lend, with **C**all **M**oney being the **C**ommon short-term loan.\n\n---\n\n### Secondary\/Other Functions of Commercial Banks\n\nBeyond their primary roles, commercial banks perform several **secondary** or **ancillary functions** that provide valuable services to customers and the economy.\n\n#### Agency and Utility Services\n\nBanks offer a diverse range of **agency and utility banking facilities** to their customers, acting as intermediaries or service providers.\n\n*   **Mediating Agent:** Banks can act as a mediating agent, for instance, by providing a **letter of credit** to an exporter on behalf of an importer, guaranteeing payment.\n*   **Underwriting Services:** They provide **underwriting services** to various businesses, guaranteeing the sale of securities.\n*   **Tax Payment:** Banks offer facilities for customers to pay their **tax challans**.\n*   **Safe Deposit Vaults:** For the safe storage of precious items, jewellery, and important documents, banks provide **safe requiring full proof security, banks issue **drafts** or **pay orders** on behalf of the payer.\n\n**MEMORY TIP:** **A**gency and **U**tility **S**ervices mean banks **A**ct for you (letters of
